13.
The funding proposal has not indicated whether these identified CSA practices and
technology needs have already been carried out in the target project areas by the target farmer
beneficiaries and agricultural BVC stakeholders. Nonetheless, the adoption of new or
continuation of CSA best practices and technology applications are adaptation measures and will
always conRtriisbku ttoe taod aagprtiactuilotunr iem repsaiclite nce.
1.1.3
14.
Baseline assessment: The Market Study and Stakeholder Consultation Report provided a
limited baseline assessment. Missing in these assessments are the baseline conditions of the
target farmers’ prevailing situations (e.g. specific climate impacts or challenges affecting their
livelihoods or BVC; breakdown of the types of activities carried out by the smallholder farmers;
their needs and challenges relating to agricultural finance and aspirations (be they simple or
ambitious); identified climate adaptation and mitigation measures in response to specific
thr eats; and lessons learned from the failures and successes of these climate initiatives).
15.
Proxy baseline: The CRDB, however, offers a proxy to what a BAU or baseline situation is
based on its long track record and agricultural lending facilities; it has a 45 to 50 per cent
market share in the whole of the United Republic of Tanzania. This private sector perspective
offers another view of what the farmers’, MSMEs’ and corporates’ needs and challenges are with
regard to ensuring a sustainable agricultural livelihood. Mindful of the fact that CRDB customers
represent the creditworthy segment of the climate vulnerable farmers and MSMEs, existing
studies and anecdotal evidence from the CRDB Bank staff provide useful indicative baseline
conditions on the agricultural sector priorities, agri-finance needs, losses and damages due to
crops (which could impact debt servicing), and issues and challenges facing the agricultural BVC
fro m production, processing and markets.
16.
Adaptation impact potential based on the submitted documents is Medium.
Scale: Medium
1.2 Paradigm shift potential

=== PAGE 91 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.11
Page 88
17.
As a caveat relating to the assessment of the paradigm shift, TAP finds that the paradigm
shift is dependent on the full baseline assessment and market study of the agriculture sector, the
agri-finance subsector, the targeted vulnerable farmers and entrepreneurs, and the timely
delivery of the complete package of credit, guarantee and insurance products and services of
TA CATDP.
18.
The timely availability of the full array of ARA financial products and services as well as
how these are strategically packaged for farmer and MSME loans for CSA practices and
technology needs or sustainable climate-resilient (or low-carbon) agri-businesses will
determine the feasibility of a paradigm shift for farmers, MSMEs and the agriculture sector. As
per the funding proposal, the final design and details will still be determined pending the result
of the market study on the insurance. Not having this insurance facility will impact the finance
accessibility and affordability of most vulnerable farmers and MSMEs as they or their projects
wi ll not be creditworthy or bankable.
19.
Paradigm shift potential for the targeted stakeholders (i.e. farmers, entrepreneurs and
MSMEs) is Medium.The combination of the potential access to the ARA finance products and
services along with the TA on CSA practices and technology applications will at least enhance
their absorptive, adaptive or even transformative capacities along the agricultural BVC. As to the
degree of paradigm shift, the accredited entity (AE) needs to have a complete understanding of
the full baseline of the farmers and MSMEs in the targeted agro-ecological areas of the
TA CATDP.
20.
First of all, the bottom-up approach to determining the baseline conditions of the
farmers, entrepreneurs and MSMEs in the agricultural BVC is limited and mainly viewed from
the lenses of the private sector stakeholders engaged in the agri-finance. The current
assessment lacks specific localized issues, challenges and needs, which are perennially impacted
by climate change and variability and extreme weather events, and now further exacerbated by
the COVID-19 pandemic. A general description of what the farmers do and intend to do once
they receive the loans can be gleaned from the environmental and social management
11
framework (ESMF) document (Annex 6 ), but these are not elaborated for each of the targeted
areas. In general, farmers say that they will “continue with current farming activities such as
growing paddy, beans, avocado, tea, cloves, maize etc. on the same area and will use the loan for
improving irrigation infrastructures such as the installation of drip irrigation systems,
construction of elevated water storage tanks, drilling boreholes as well as expanding their
activities by acquiring additional land in the neighbourhood”. Essentially, they plan to maintain
their existing location, not expand, and continue their current BAU activities. Information such
as this is therefore needed in improving the design of the proposed TACATDP interventions to
en sure a paradigm shift.
21.
Similarly, a baseline assessment of the agriculture finance industry in each of the agro-
12
ecological zones is not available; only national or general assessments from the literature
13
reviews, from the perspective of the CRDB , and partners of the TACATDP project have been
presented. The funding proposal has not adequately presented the existing agriculture finance
systems from both the formal and informal sources in these target areas. The details in terms of
available agri-financial products and services (e.g. traditional, Islamic, private, formal and
informal); eligible financing purposes; interest rates; tenors; manner of payment of debt;
collateral and valuation of collateral; processes; fees and costs and other conditions; financing
gaps; needs; and challenges.
11
Funding proposal, Annex 7, Appendix 10, para. 2.
12
Funding proposal, Annex 2, citing Technical Workshop on Needs-Based Climate Finance for East Africa 19-20
February 2020 Zanzibar, United Republic of Tanzania.
13
Funding proposal, Annex 2.

=== PAGE 92 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.11
Page 89
22.
However, as ARA finance products and services will be the first of their kind in the
finance sector in the United Republic of Tanzania, the success of TACATDP will depend on the
ability of CRBD to demonstrate the de-risking of the lending transactions in the agriculture
sector and the de-risking of the climate-sensitive agricultural activities, crops, BVCs and
markets. This success can trigger the replication of the CRDB climate operationalization best
practices under the TACATDP by other third party FIs as led by FSDT. This could lead to the
creation of new climate-linked financial products and services (similar to ARA) that could
strategically address the climate change risks and opportunities (e.g. carbon markets,
greenhouse gas mitigation), not just in the agricultural BVC but also in other allied industries
and sectors (e.g. manufacturing, transport, energy). The development and introduction of
parametric insurance or climate insurance also would jumpstart the initiatives on tailoring
various climate risk mitigation schemes to manage the risks to other food or strategic export
crops. The ARA Guarantee Facility, while also a contributory factor to the paradigm shift, should
14
be fully explored further during and at the end of the programme; going without it stymies the
three-pronged approach of the ARA suite of services at the end of the programme. Overall, the
pa radigm shift for the agri-financial sector is clear and rated high.
23.
The paradigm shift for the government is Medium. The success of TACATDP also
depends on the effective provision of TA on the targeted mainstreaming of climate in the
operations and decision-making process of the participating government agencies and divisions:
Ministry of Agriculture, the Tanzania Insurance Regulatory Authority and the Tanzania
Meteorological Authority. This approach will enhance the capacity of each of the government
15
organizations to operationalize CSA plans and strategies, a process which has been stymied by
government budgetary and development funding constraints. The paradigm shift potential for
the government is in its ability to improve the policy and regulatory environment based on
actual bottom-up approaches with the private sector and agricultural stakeholders working on
the transformation of the agricultural BVC.
24.
The paradigm shift for the agriculture sector is Medium. Overall ,this paradigm shift will
clearly occur based on the efficiency and effectiveness of each of the above interventions
focusing on (i) the behavioural change of the farmers moving from traditional or BAU farming to
CSA; (ii) the provision of ARA finance and services to support CSA practices and technology
needs alongside capacity-building on climate and the use of climate information services for
crop production and investment decision-making; and (iii) government policy and regulatory
interventions along the agricultural BVC based on the bottom-up collaborative experience with
the pri vateR seepctloicra. bility for knowledge dissemination and behavioural change
1.2.1
25.

establishment of an online interface and training support for the CRDB climate risks assessment
too l) are highly replicable amongst the FIs.

27.
The risk to achieving the paradigm shift relates to the degree of shift that will occur.
could be from increasing absortpive capacities, or enhancement of adaptive to transformative
capacities. This is due to the limited baseline data and information on the targeted stakeholders,
crops, critical resources, built infrastructures and investments (agriculture financing sector,
MSME BVC) in agriculture crop production and BVC; there is a need to ascertain the baseline
level of farming and lending activities in the project areas, for example, to be able to compare
the farming and lending shift due to the TACATDP interventions. While the AE has uniquely
presented a credible sampling of the baseline market given its 45 to 50 per cent agriculture
market share, the AE, as it moves forward, still needs to present updated and expanded
evidence-based information on the market, and stakeholder consultation amongst farmers.
These would validate the market needs as they relate to (i) the terms and conditions of the
proposed financial products and serivces relating to credit, guarantees and parametric
insurance; (ii) the choice of CSA crops, inputs, technologies and practices; (iii) the
documentation requirements needed in processing loans, insurance and payouts between
farmers and CRDB or other FIs; and (iv) how the COVID-19 pandemic will exacerbate the
cli mate impact on the present and future sustainability of the agriculture BVC.
28.
In view of the above discussions and balancing the overall impact potential and risks,
TAP finds that the paradigm shift potential is Medium on the basis of the available information.
Scale: High
1. 3 Sustainable development potential
29.
Sustainable development finance and financial inclusion: TACATDP offers an innovative
set of financial products and services which directly address climate-related risks, issues on
financial availability, affordability, and accessibility, and financial exclusion. TACATDP is also a
financial inclusion mechanism that targets poor vulnerable farmers and entrepreneurs along the
climate-sensitive agricultural BVC which have no or minimal banking experience. Thus, the
programme aims to protect them from the risk of onerous lending schemes or predatory loans
fro m informal sources.
16
30.
Economic co-benefits: TACATDP is a facility that will help jumpstart the grassroots
agriculture-based economy. The timely provision of the ARA facilities is expected to infuse
much-needed finance amongst the agriculture stakeholders that are suffering from the impacts
of climate change, which are now exacerbated by the economic lockdown due to COVID-19
containment measures. The ARA facilities’ array of flexible financing structures will help address
the funding needs for farmers’ CSA practices and technologies to improve yields and boost crop
sales; the indicative affordable or competitive rates will help offset the high cost of operations
(farm inputs, logistics); the flexible payment terms to match the farmers’ trading cycles will
en able them to ensure food on the table and stable livelihoods.
31.
Gender mainstreaming: The funding proposal has included a Gender Action Plan (GAP).
The GAP seeks to mainstream gender in the TACATDP from project design to implementation. A
minimum target of 40 to 50 per cent of the TACATDP beneficiaries are women. The GAP will
include the accessibility of the ARA facilities to women farmers or women-owned MSMEs (under
Component 1) and the participation of women in the TA capacity-building activities, workshops,
17
tra ining and consultations under Component 2.
32.
Environmental co-benefits: The funding proposal has included a safeguards package in
the preparation of the TACATDP and is in environment and social safeguards (ESS) Category B.
Among the frameworks prepared are the ESMF and the resettlement policy framework (RPF).
16
See FP Section D.3. Sustainable Development.
17
FP Annex 8: Gender Action Plan.

33.
The ESMF has flagged eight crops that have potential risks on the environment and
society. These crops include cashew, cocoa, coffee, cotton, maize, sesame seed, sugar cane and
wheat. The funding proposal needs to safeguard against the following risks: child labour (for
coffee), terrestial biodiversity (for cashews, maize, sesame seed, sugar cane, wheat), freshwater
biodiversity (for cocoa, coffee, maize, sugar cane) and protected or conservation areas (for
18
cotton, maize, sesame seeds, sugar cane). The ESMF contains an ESS report. As per the ESS
report, “The ESMF provides guidance on how environmental and social aspects of TACATDP
shall be identified, assessed and managed. It provides a framework to assist CRDB in screening
subprojects at the planning stage and institute measures to address adverse impacts. According
to the GCF Environmental and Social Policy requirement, the TACATDP has been assigned
Environmental Category B and CRDB will not fund any subproject which will fall under category
19
A o r which will cause involuntary resettlement.”
34.
The RPF is made available “to screen all subprojects for their potential land acquisition
impacts and provide all the necessary procedures to follow in mitigating and minimizing
20
involuntary resettlement associated with the proposed TACATDP.” It is assumed that the
“subprojects under the proposed TACATDP will be taking place on already owned farms, plots
or pieces of land and areas reserved for agriculture with no or little need of resettlement with
less significant for economic activities displacement.” However, in case the TACATDP
subprojects involve the “acquisition of land required for construction work of storage for crops
or water, installation of solar based systems, production of bio and organic pesticides or for
ex pansion of irrigation canals, the RPF is ready”.
35.
Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs): The TACATDP can be linked to other SDGs.
21
Among these are: No Poverty (SDG 1), Zero Hunger (SDG 2), Gender Equality (SDG 5),
Affordable and Clean Energy (SDG 7), Decent Work and Economic Growth (SDG 8), Industry,
Innovatio. n and Infrastructure (SDG 9), Reduced Inequalities (SDG 10), Responsible
Consumption and Production (SDG 12), Life on Land (SDG 15) and Partnerships for the Goals
(SD G 17)
36.
Risk to sustainable development: The failure to monitor numerous smallholder farmer
act ivities may lead to environmental degradation or maladaptation.
37.
Overall, the sustainable development potential of the project is High.
Scale: Medium
1. 4 Needs of the recipient
38.
Needs of the country: As stated in the intended nationally determined contribution
(INDC) of the United Republic of Tanzania, the country needs to improve agriculture via
“increasing yields through inter alia climate-resilient agriculture”; “protecting smallholder
farmers against climate-related shocks including through crop insurance”; and “strengthening
22
knowledge, extension services and agricultural infrastructure to target climate action”.
However, the lack of a government budget means that it is not possible to fully implement these
plans and strategies (e.g. National Climate Change Strategy, Agriculture Climate Resilience
23
Plans). The TACATDP specifically addresses these issues, especially crop insurance and
increasing crop yields under Components 1 and 2, among others. TACATDP Components 1 and 2
addresses these.
18
FP Annex 6: ESMF – Appendix 8 - ENVIRONMENTAL AND SOCIAL RISK MANAGEMENT REPORT FOR TANZANIA
COMMODITIES.
19
FP Annex 6: ESS Disclosure Report.

FP Annex 6: RPF.

Funding proposal, Annex 7: CRDB Final Stakeholder Engagement Survey Report.

=== PAGE 95 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.11
Page 92
39.
Needs of the farmers, entrepreneurs and corporates: As per the funding proposal, the
farmers and corporates commonly need access to financial products to acquire technologies that
will increase their climate-adaptive and climate-resilient capacities. Corporates need financial
products to increase the competitiveness of farming businesses. Smallholder farmers, on the
other hand, need loans (which have flexible repayment schedules or grace periods, acceptable
collateral schemes, need for guarantors, or interest-free types for Muslims) that will help them
finance crop production (e.g. paddy, cloves, maize, avocado, tea and other crops) to meet market
demand. Farmers also need financing that supports small-scale rice processing mills and
improves irrigation infrastructures (e.g. drip irrigation systems, elevated water storage tanks,
boreholes) as these will help improve the yields, quality, and salability of their crops in the
24
ma rket. The TACATDP ARA suite of facilities address these needs under Component 1.
40.
Market needs from the CRDB perspective: Having a 45 to 50 per cent market share of the
agriculture finance sector, CRDB has an average portfolio of USD 219 million per year, and its
total portfolio increased 13 per cent during the period 2017 to 2020; this increase in agriculture
loan value shows a stable 0.61 per cent growth. Within the agriculture portfolio itself, one can
infer interesting changes that show an emerging trend; the percentage change of the loan
portfolio beginning in 2017 and ending in 2020 shows that corporate loans declined by 7.08 per
cent; SME loans increased by 67.47 per cent, and microfinance loans leapt by 4565.01 per cent.
Analysing the loans, the top crops (86 per cent of the loan portfolio) are tobacco, sugar, cotton,
coffee, hemp, avocado, cashew and rice. According to CRBD, it does see and project an increase
in demand from small farm holders and small and medium-sized enterprise (SME) loan growth
in the future. TACATDP addresses the financing needs of this market segment.
41.
Needs of the financing sector: The financing sector needs a system by which to de-risk
lending transactions with the farmers and entrepreneurs and de-risk the agricultural activities
their clients/borrowers participate in; the FI decision-makers need to understand how the
climate impacts the production, post-production and markets and what policy or regulatory
interventions are needed to address these. TACATDP squarely addresses these under
Co mponents 1 and 2.
42.
Considering the above, the TAP rates the needs of the recipient as Medium.
Scale: Medium

Strong ownership and support amongst programme partners: Led by the CRDB, the

45.
CRDB is a credible private sector partner for GCF. As per the funding propsal market
study in Annex 2, it presently has 20 per cent of its portfolio lent to the agriculture sector, which
is equivalent to a 45 to 50 per cent market share of agriculture lending in the United Republic of
Tanzania. CRDB is currently the largest commercial bank since its inception in 1947 as the Land
Bank of Tanganyika. It was subsequently restructured into Tanzania Rural Development Bank,
35
and then CRDB. CRDB has a good track record in lending to the agriculture sector. As of
December 2020, 12.77 per cent of CRDB’s total loans come from agriculture loans and amount to
TZS 528,400,494,602.00; within the agriculture portfolio, corporate loans amount to 83.45 per
cent, SMEs loans amount to 16.05 per cent, and microfinance loans amount to 0.50 per cent. For
the period 2017 to 2020, the four-year average loan exposure in agriculture is at TZS
470,105,281,867; an average of 86.41 per cent comes from the corporate accounts, 13.35 per
cen t from SMEs, and 0.24 per cent from microfinance loans.
46.
CRDB is partnering with the National Insurance Corporation (NIC). NIC is a government-
owned insurance company in the United Republic of Tanzania, which has experience in piloting
parametric insurance schemes in the agriculture sector. CRDB is in the process of extending the
existing memorandum of understanding (MOU) with NIC covering traditional insurance to cover
pa rametric insurance as well.

36

37

PASS is a guarantee facility to support growth and investments in commercial agriculture and
BVCs. The financial products and services offered by PASS include traditional guarantees,
38
po rtfolio guarantees and institutional guarantees.

1.6 EffOicvieernviceyw a nd effectiveness
1.6.1
56.
GCF will allocate USD 100 million for TACATDP; 70 per cent of this will be in the form of
credit, 20 per cent in grants, and 10 per cent as a guarantee. This will be matched by USD 100
million from CRDB, resulting in a GCF to private sector leverage ratio of 1 in terms of total
funding. However, this USD 100 million will only be used in the proposed ARA Credit Facility to
match the GCF USD 70 million loan, leading to a GCF to CRDB leverage ratio of 0.7.
41
Sokoine University Graduates Entrepreneur Cooperative.

AE Response to ITAP Questions Batch 1.

=== PAGE 98 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.11
Page 95
57.
Eighty-nine per cent will be used for Component 1 (USD 89.48 million of GCF total
funding), and this is broken down as follows: 70 per cent for the establishment the ARA Credit
Facility (USD 70 million of total funding); 10 per cent for the ARA Guarantee Facility (at USD 10
million of total funding); and 9 per cent for the ARA parametric insurance (at USD 9.48 million of
total funding). GCF grant finance will be used for Activity 1.3 (Establishment of an insurance
sch eme dedicated to ARA for smallholder farmers).
58.
The combination of financial products and services will be effective in de-risking the
financial transactions between CRDB and the agriculture-based borrowers. All creditworthy
stakeholders looking to fund short, medium or long-term viable investments in transformative
CSA activities can access the ARA Credit Facility that matches their needs in terms of rates and
tenors. Other transformative but financially risky CSA financing needs from vulnerable farmers
will be enhanced by the ARA Guarantee Facility and/or parametric insurance products. Thus,
the effectiveness of this ARA Facility to grant credit especially to the target vulnerable farmers
an d MSME entrepreneurs comes as a package and not as standalone facilities.
59.
The ARA Credit Facility will be handled by CRDB and through its nationwide, 260 branch
43
network, in collaboration with the Agriculture Marketing Cooperatives and Societies (AMCOS).
This will make it available to the targeted base of stakeholders mainly composed of small and
medium-sized farm holders (representing 73 per cent of total beneficiaries), SMEs (26 per cent)
44
and Corporates (1 per cent). In general, the ARA Credit Facility will offer short-term loans (6
months or 1 to 2 years) for CSA activities and technology needs of farmers; medium-term loans
(5 years); and long-term corporate loans (10 years) to finance infrastructure investments, agri-
processing, service providers and asset financing. CRDB will maintain its practice to structure
45
the loans with different tenors depending on business cash flow; these practices include
“tailored financial products to meet their needs such as aggregation and debt mutualization;
leasing contracts for cooperatives primarily through AMCOS, pay-as-you-buy options as well as
the promotion of low-cost and high quality locally manufactured technologies (e.g. net shading
46
and polyhouses).” The CRDB infrastructure will most likely be effective and efficient in
rea ching out to the targeted stakeholders under this programme.
60.
ARA parametric insurance: The ARA Insurance Facility will be developed and managed
by CRDB in partnership/collaboration with insurance companies. TAP requested clarification on
what entity will be handling the underwriting risk. CRDB stated that “CRDB will not underwrite
the insurance itself but will partner with a local insurance provider with expertise to manage
said risks. CRDB will explore, report, and account for these insurance risks when conducting the
47
insurance market studies and finalizing the scheme’s ultimate design.” CRDB will work closely
with insurance companies that have signed MOUs with them. These arrangements will help and
guide CRDB in the establishment of major design frameworks, terms and conditions of the
proposed parametric insurance (e.g. crop coverage, eligible farmers/policyholders, policy
premiums, manner of claims payment, selection of 8 eligible agro-ecological zones, etc). Among
the key design features that need to be addressed will be the need for client and portfolio
diversification as aligned with the CRDB credit policy, mitigation of risk of simultaneous payouts
to farmers that could be triggered by low yields due to the impacts of extreme climate events,
and sharing of confidential data and information with relevant government agencies in aid of
policy and regulatory initiatives. “Additionally, the insurance portfolio will be distributed to 8
selected agro-ecological zones in the United Republic of Tanzania to avoid concentration on a
single geographical area. The credit policy of the bank guides the diversification of its loan
portfolio into different sectors and subsectors to avoid the risk of over-concentration into single

62.
The TA component will specifically support the development and implementation of the
ARA facilities under Component 1 and in general the mainstreaming of CSA in the finance and
agricultural sectors. Seven per cent (USD 7.0345 million in grant funds) will be used to finance
five capacity-building and five policymaking initiatives under Component 2 (Policy

These activities will also support the ARA activities under Component 1. Other cost items (at

(b)
Economic and financial analysis:The funding proposal does not include projects at this
stage. In lieu of this, it has identified a set of eligible funding activities under the TACATDP for
which an economic and financial analysis has been conducted to determine eligible and possibly
via ble CSA activities.
63.
From an economic development perspective, the identified CSA interventions offer
significant benefits including the externalities. The top CSA interventions are demi-lunes (rank
#1), SF (rank #2), AC (rank #3), RWHSS (rank #4), SRI (rank #5); then 2 CSAs which have Cost
Benefit Ratios (CBR) between 1 and 2 are IC (rank #6) and DI (rank #7). Given the risky nature
of agriculture and the profiles of targeted borrowers amongst farmers and SMEs, the most
financially viable ARA interventions for CRBD are demi-lunes/AFM (internal rate of return (IRR)
of 106 per cent and Payback Period (PP) of 0.85), AC (IRR of 83 per cent, PP of 1.5), SF (IRR of
75 per cent, PP of 1.5), RWHSS (IRR of 44 per cent, PP of 1.5), IC and DI (IRR of 9.5 per cent, PP
of 3.25) and SRI (IRR of 6 per cent, PP of 4.2).
64.
Net present value drawbacks: The funding proposal analysis of the costs may not have
covered a more in depth analysis of the costs, especially those that have been impacted by
climate change and COVID-19 disruptions. Among such operating costs are land preparation,
personnel costs (labour), seed costs, fertilization costs, disease and pest control costs and other
co ntingency costs.
65.
Weak budget and implementation/procurement plans: The funding proposal’s budget
plan adds up but more details are needed on the programme’s target beneficiaries, intended
subactivities and alignment with the implementation plans and procurement strategies. These
items need to be improved upon to demonstrate complimentarity or support and thus provide
an impression of the efficiency and effectiveness of the funding proposal execution and delivery
of the intended outputs.
66.
Budget plan: As submitted, the funding proposal’s Annex 4 – Detailed Budget Plan does
not provide enough details on the budget items (e.g. components of the automated weather
stations (Activity 1.3-A7); number of target farmers and MSME beneficiaries per workshop per
year (Activity 2.2). In addition, some entries are missing (e.g. for computer-assisted survey
information collection CASIC) and some do not match (e.g. Activity 2.2 and K Budget figure and
budget note 2K). These need to be filled in to ensure the clarity of the budget plan overall. While
the funding proposal already has the details, these are not properly reflected in the budget

=== PAGE 100 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.11
Page 97
67.
Implementation plan: As submitted, the funding proposal (Annex 5: Implementation
Timetable) needs to provide adequately detailed timelines for the subactivities under
Co mponents 1 and 2; this raises the risk of poor implementation of the proposed activities.
68.
Procurement plan: As submitted, the funding proposal (Annex 10: Procurement Plan)
has presented procurement plans for consultancy assignments with selection methods and a
time schedule. Missing are budget items on equipment (i.e. AWS). This needs to be
syc hronizedwith the proposed activities.
69.
The budget plan details for Component 1 show that the suite of the CRBD ARA facilities
will all be launched in year 1.48 Similarly, the assumptions and financial analysis simulate the
first-year simultaneous launching of the credit, guarantee and insurance. However, the project
implementation plan (Annex 5) shows that the process is not simultaneous, but sequential. The
ARA Credit Facility establishment occurs during Q1 and Q2 in Year 1, the expansion of the ARA
Guarantee coverage to support the ARA Credit Facility (for farmers and MSMEs) takes place
during Q3 and Q4 of Year 1 and Q1 to Q4 of Year 2, and the establishment of ARA Parametric
Insurance takes place during Q1 to Q4 in Year 2. The tight procurement process for experts on
adaptation, parametric insurance, sustainable business devleopment and market studies will
take four months (almost two quarters) in Year 1. Similarly, the budget plan details for
Component 2 show that the planned activities (i.e. 2.1, 2.2, 2.3, 2.4, 2.5, 2.6, 2.7, 2.8, 2.9 and 2.10)
are not consistent with the implementation plans. These factors will impact the expected results
un der E5 project performance indicators.
70.
The implementation of the ARA Parametric Insurance (e.g. area yield insurance for
maize) will be completed at the end of Year 2. However, the risk of delay in developing this
ins urance facility is also high given that the following activities need to be done first:
(a)
These include the assessment of eligible land in hectares to be covered per farmer;
(b)
Another is the installation of AWS in the target project areas, which also depends on the
nu mber of eligible hectares of land per AWS; and
(c)
Technical details on standards, compliance with regulatory permits and where and how
the AWS will be located, constructed, operated and maintained, and coordinated with National
Hy dromet Agencies are not available.
71.
Thus, the farmers and MSMEs looking to enhance their credit worthiness will only enjoy
this climate risk protection in Q1 of Year 3 at the earliest.Thus, the proposed parametric
insurance will only have an effective three-year period for implementation versus the five-year
pe riod claimed under the funding proposal. CRDB countered that it will be available in a year.49
72.
The Component 2 budget allocation schedule needs to match the implementation plan
(i.e. for activities 2.1 to 2.10), and this needs to be improved by adding a subactivity breakdown
into the implementation plan. It needs to show clear sequencing and/or logic on the delivery of
identified outputs and main activities that could support Component 1, for example. Activity 2.1,
which is the “development of an online interface and training support for the CRDB climate risks
assessment tool”, is slated to begin in Q1 of Year 3 versus the Year 1 schedule as per the budget
timeline. Activity 2.1 comes three years late and will miss out on an opportunity to support the
mainstreaming of climate change in the investment operations as the suite of ARA facilities is
launched in the beginning of Year 1 ( for the Credit and Guarantee Facilities) and Year 2 (for
insurance). The capacity-building component is also targeting Years 3 and 5 in contrast to the
budget plan’s yearly schedule of “CRDB and cooperative staff training on integration climate
risks and considerations within regular banking operations.” The budget plan also fails to detail
the scope of the capacity-buildi ng effort in terms of number of CRDB staff, branches, in the
48
Funding proposal, Annex 4: Detailed Budget Plan. Component 1.

country. Activity 2.1 is supposed to cover the “Prioritization of risk-reducing and climate
adaptation investments, increase availability of climate data analytics, and capacity-building of
50

1. 7 Baseline assessments
73.
The baseline assessment of farmer and MSME stakeholders needs to be carried out
covering livelihoods, priority crops, farming practices from traditional to CSA (e.g. Zai
measures), technologies in use (e.g. smartphones for weather and news reports, solar-powered
instruments for argriculture use), ecosystem resources (e.g. water, soil), assets and investments
(e.g. land, farming equipment, inputs, crops in warehouses), built infrastructure (e.g. irrigation,
roads, power, cold storage facilities), issues, challenges, and needs in the agriculture production
and post-production BVC. The study needs to cover the impacts of climate change, COVID-19
and sustainable finance, the latter focusing on finance availability (e.g. finance credit/supplier
credit lines), accessibility (e.g. credit profile, loan processing, collateral requirements),
affordabilty (interest rates, loan tenors) and acceptability (e.g. Islamic finance vs. conventional
ba nking finance products).
74.
The baseline assessement of the agricultural finance subsector needs to be carried out
for the current agricultural finance market covering the various agri-finance related products
and services offered by formal sources (e.g. FIs, bank and non-bank FIs) and informal sources
(e.g. private money lenders, community loans, etc); evidence-based comparative baseline anlysis
of the financial products or services; interest rates for available tenors; eligible financing
activities, collateral, collateral valuation, guarantees, insurance, fees and costs, and regualtory
bo dies overseeing the particular FI or non-bank FIs, and relevant laws.
75.
Market study: A market study on the proposed financial products and services (i.e.
credits, guarantees and parametric insurance) needs to be completed as a whole. Study should
co ver demand and willingness to pay.
76.
Overall, based on the availabe information submitted and the need to further prepare
the funding proposal, the effectiveness and efficiency is rated as medium.

Reply to the Independent Technical Advisory Panel assessment
findings
Proposal name: Tanzania Agriculture Climate Adaptation Technology Deployment
Formerly Tanzania’s “Cooperative and Rural Development
Programme (TACATDP)
Bank”,
CRDB Bank Plc,
Accredited entity:
Medium Category
Impact potential
We took note of the iTAP assessment finding on the Impact Potential scoring it as a “Medium”. The
programme will invest only in ‘fit for purpose’ adaptation and resilience measures to address climate
risks in the agriculture sector (cropping sector) in the face of current climate variability and future
climate change over the project’s operational life. Prioritized measures will ensure that beneficiary
farmer operators will not harm the resilience of the defined system they operate within. The project seeks
to invest only in ‘fit for purpose’ adaptation and resilience measures to address climate risks in the
agriculture sector (cropping sector) in the face of current climate variability and future climate change
over the project’s operational life. Prioritized measures will ensure that beneficiary farmer operators will
Paradigm shift potential
not harm the resilience of the defined system they operate within.
We took note of the iTAP assessment findings ranking this category as “Medium”. This proposal addresses
key barriers hampering the mobilization of climate investments at scale to support the adaptation of the
agriculture sector in Tanzania. In recent years, private sector agriculture operations and agribusinesses
have received relatively low investment due to perceived risks in the sector.
We believe TACATDP has an enorneous potential to transform the country's agricultural sector while
building its resilience to climatic variabilities and the adverse effects of climate change. The Pro gramme
will strengthen the financial and technical capacity of both the financial sector and of the farmers, a
Ssiutustaatiionna bwlhei dche vweillol pinm tuernnt spuoptpeonrtti alilvelihoods, health and the sustainability of the environment.
We appreciate that ITAP finds the sustainable development potential of the project is "High", as it intends
to address the grassroots agriculture-based economy. The timely provision of ARA Facilities should infuse
much-needed finance amongst the agriculture stakeholders suffering from climate change impacts.
Needs of the recipient
We appreciate ITAP rating this category as "Medium". However, there is a much-needed financing gap to
unlock the climate resilience of smallholder farmers and transform the adaptive capacity of Tanzania's
agriculture sector. CRDB is Tanzania's largest lender to the agriculture sector (45% of all credit to
agriculture borrowers) and therefore has a huge role to play and huge expectations from the market.
CRDB needs a system to de-risk the lending transactions and agriculture activities with farmers and
entrepreneurs.
We believe this programme will transform agriculture in Tanzania and ensure the country progressively
transitions to a more resilient system and people.

GCF/B.30/02/Add.11
Page 105
Country ownership
This programme has strong ownership and support amongst program partners, as indicated by ITAP.
Again, we appreciate ranking this category as ‘Medium”. CRDB has a comprehensive set of partners
ranging from public to the private sectors that have expressed their support and commitment for
TACATDP. We believe this programme has support at the highest level from all stakeholders in the
country.
Efficiency and effectiveness
We appreciate ITAP rating this category as “Medium”. We shall extend our market study on the proposed
financial products and services, i.e., credit, guarantee, and parametric insurance. The market study should
cover demand and willingness to pay to ensure that the combination of financial products and services
will de-risk the financial transactions between CRDB and the agriculture-based borrowers geared for
adaptation measures.
Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel:
CRDB agrees to implement the conditions presented by ITAP.

Gender Analysis and Assessment

Page 109
Summary
This Gender Analysis and Assessment for the Tanzania Agriculture Climate Adaptation Technology
Deployment Programme (TACATDP) is divided into four sections:
1 - The first section “Gender institutional, legal and policy frameworks and programmes” provides an
overview of the institutional, legal and policy frameworks and programmes for gender in Tanzania. It
underlines the alignment of the country with international agreements and its efforts to integrate
gender considerations in national policies and sectorial policies (agriculture, climate change, gender,
etc.).
2 - The second section “Gender overview, barriers and challenges in Tanzania” provides the core
assessment and analysis of gender aspects in Tanzania. It highlights gender inequalities across sectors,
especially through the review of inequalities in employment. It further emphasizes on inequalities
faced by women working in agriculture around four main barriers:
(i) Access to land and land ownership;
(ii) Access to labor and time available for work;
(iii) Technical and technological barriers, and;
(iv) Financial barriers.
3 - The third section “Actions and projects to reduce gender unbalance in Tanzania” presents actions
and projects implemented, ongoing, or in preparation to reduce the gender gap in Tanzania. This
section promotes CRDB Bank as an active stakeholder in the fight for gender equality.
4 - The last section presents the “Problem Tree Analysis” developed for the “Tanzania Agriculture
Climate Adaptation Technology Deployment Programme” project in compliance with the Green
Climate Fund’s requirements. This section explains which barriers and challenges (identified in section
two, however not yet addressed in the country) will be addressed by the project. It shows that gender
equality lies at the heart of this project, that will help remove barriers for women in agriculture.

=== PAGE 113 ===
Page 110
Introduction
This gender assessment was performed under the Project Preparation Facility (PPF) for Tanzania
Agriculture Climate Adaptation Technology Deployment Programme (TACATDP) and seeks to present
the issues, gaps and problems that should be addressed by gender-responsive project interventions.
This document provides a high-level overview of the gender situation and key gender matters in
Tanzania, more specifically gender issues relevant to the TACATDP.
The Green Climate Fund (GCF) has elaborated guidelines on Mainstreaming Gender in Green Climate
Fund Projects with UN Women to support the integration of gender equality in climate change
interventions and climate finance. Gender mainstreaming is central to GCF objectives and guiding
principles, and a gender-sensitive approach is embedded in the GCF Governing Instrument and related
policies, which have been adopted since the Fund’s inception.
Gender responsive approaches are essential to encouraging a paradigm shift towards low-emission
and climate-resilient development pathways, including decisions and actions by women and men [1].
In accordance with the GCF Gender Policy and Action Plan, the core elements of gender mainstreaming
required in GCF project design are a gender analysis, gendered actions and gender-sensitive
Monitoring and Evaluation. Gender aspects need to be mainstreamed at project identification level
with the involvement of men and women to discuss the project idea, and analyze their roles and
responsibilities, and at project proposal formulation stage by reflecting gender issues across proposed
activities and indicators, based on the collection of sex-disaggregated data, and with the support of
gender experts. Gender analysis is at the core of mainstreaming gender considerations in the project
cycle and helps reveal the significance of existing gender inequalities and gaps in relation to climate
change, and the potential contributions of women and men to climate action.
The following gender analysis and assessment is aligned with GCF requirements of:
- Identifying and analyzing gender issues relevant to the project;
- Reporting findings of country/regional gender diagnostics and undertaking project-specific
analysis;
- Reflecting the results of consultations on the project objectives or components with relevant
stakeholders – including women, girls, men and boys in the project area, gender advocates
and women’s civil society organizations, local and national authorities;
- Conducting a problem tree analysis to highlight how the project addresses the barriers and
challenges identified.

1. Gender institutional, legal and policy frameworks and programmes
1.1. Gender international frameworks and programmes ratified by Tanzania
Tanzania has ratified both the 2030 Sustainable Development Goals (SDG) Agenda, among which SDG5
focuses on Gender Equality, and the long term 2063 Agenda promoting “full gender equality in all life-
spheres” among other goals.
Tanzania has also ratified key international instruments driven toward the fight for gender balancing
and integration such as the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against
Women (1979) and the Beijing Platform for Action (1995). Finally, the Lima Work Programme on
Gender (2014) provides general guidance on gender aspects at international level.
The regulatory landscape is graphically represented in below chart:
1.1.1. Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (1979)
The Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) has been
adopted in 1979 by the United Nations General Assembly and is often described as an international
bill of rights for women. Consisting of a preamble and 30 articles, it defines what constitutes

--- Table 1 on Page 114 ---
Tanzania has ratified both the 2030 Sustainable Development Goals (SDG) Agenda, among which SDG5
focuses on Gender Equality, and the long term 2063 Agenda promoting “full gender equality in all life-
spheres” among other goals.

--- Table 2 on Page 114 ---
Tanzania has also ratified key international instruments driven toward the fight for gender balancing
and integration such as the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against
Women (1979) and the Beijing Platform for Action (1995). Finally, the Lima Work Programme on
Gender (2014) provides general guidance on gender aspects at international level.

=== PAGE 115 ===
Page 112
discrimination against women and sets up an agenda for national action to end such discrimination.
Discrimination against women is stated as follows:
"...any distinction, exclusion or restriction made on the basis of sex which has the effect or
purpose of impairing or nullifying the recognition, enjoyment or exercise by women, irrespective
of their marital status, on a basis of equality of men and women, of human rights and
fundamental freedoms in the political, economic, social, cultural, civil or any other field" [2].
By accepting the Convention, Tanzania commits to undertake a series of measures to end
discrimination against women in all forms, including:
- To incorporate the principle of equality of men and women in their legal system, abolish all
discriminatory laws and adopt appropriate ones prohibiting discrimination against women;
- To establish tribunals and other public institutions to ensure the effective protection of women
against discrimination; and
- To ensure elimination of all acts of discrimination against women by persons, organizations or
enterprises.
Having ratified this Convention, Tanzania is legally bound to put its provisions into practice. It is also
committed to submit national reports, at least every four years, on measures it has taken to comply
with treaty obligations. Tanzania has submitted its last report in 2014 [3].
1.1.2. Fourth World Conference on Women - Beijing Platform for Action (1995)
The Beijing Platform for Action came out of the Fourth World Conference on Women held in Beijing in
1995. Among its statement, “The Platform for Action emphasizes that women share common concerns
that can be addressed only by working together and in partnership with men towards the common goal
of gender equality around the world. It respects and values the full diversity of women's situations and
conditions and recognizes that some women face particular barriers to their empowerment” [4].
The Beijing Platform sets strategic objectives and actions that have served as guidance for Tanzania to
establish its national strategies and plans, such as the 2008 National Strategy for Gender Development:
- Women and Poverty;
- Education and Training of Women;
- Women and Health;
- Violence against Women;
- Women and Armed Conflict;
- Women and the Economy;
- Women in Power and Decision-making;
- Institutional Mechanism for the Advancement of Women;
- Human Rights of Women;
- Women and the Media;
- Women and the Environment;
- The Girl-child.
1.1.3. Lima Work Programme on Gender (2014)
In 2014, the Conference of Parties (COP) established the first Lima Work Programme on Gender
(LWPG) [5] to advance gender balance and integrate gender considerations into the work of Parties
and the Secretariat in implementing the Convention and the Paris Agreement so as to achieve gender

responsive climate policy and action. COP22 (2016) decided on a three-year extension of the LWPG
and COP25 (2019) Parties agreed to a 5-year Enhanced Lima Work Programme on Gender and its
Gender Action Plan. The Enhanced Gender Action Plan sets out objectives and activities under five
priority areas, twenty activities and thirty-five outputs. They aim to advance knowledge and
understanding of gender-responsive climate action and its coherent mainstreaming. The five priority
areas are the following:
- Capacity-building, knowledge management and communication;
- Gender balance, participation and women’s leadership;
- Coherence;
- Gender-responsive implementation and means of implementation;
- Monitoring and reporting.
1.2. Gender national frameworks and programmes
The first integration of gender aspects in a ministry happened in 1992 with the creation of the Ministry
of Community Development, Women’s Affairs and Children, established as the national machinery
dealing with women affairs. The current Ministry of Health, Community Development, Gender, Elderly
and Children (MoHCDGEC) appeared in 2016. It is responsible for spearheading gender equality and
the empowerment of women.
The MoHCDGEC is a fully-fledged ministry with a mandate for overseeing the implementation of
Government policies and strategies as well as coordinating and monitoring implementation of these
polices for gender equality and women’s empowerment at different levels. The ministry lacks financial
and human resources despite improvements over the last years (creation of posts and increase in the
access to financial resources), preventing it from being fully operational and refraining it from properly
implementing established activities.
The MoHCDGEC is not the only structure fighting for gender balancing but remains the principle and
overarching one. Indeed, as stated in the National Strategy for Gender Development (2008), sector
ministries, central ministries, regional and district levels, the private sector, local communities and
development partners also play an important role [6], [7].
The Government of Tanzania has adopted policies that advance gender equality and youth inclusion in
a supportive enabling environment. In 2000 and 2004, the Government has amended the 1977
Constitution to increase women’s participation in the National Parliament and Local Authorities. The
Constitution of the United Republic of Tanzania now contains Article 9 providing a juridical foundation
for gender equality in Tanzania, identifying people as being equal and free individuals enjoying
freedom, justice, fraternity and concord. It also stipulates that the State authority and all its Agencies
are obliged to direct their policies and programmes toward ensuring this outcome [8].
The Government also formulated the Women and Gender Development Policy (2000) and National
Strategy for Gender Development (2008) to further emphasize gender development and integration
and to align the country with international frameworks. Further than pure gender policies, the gender
dimension is also integrated in several national plans, programmes and strategies such as:
- The Tanzania Development Vision 2025 (1999);
- The Agriculture Climate Resilience Plan 2014 – 2019 (2014);
- The National Five-Year Development Plan 2016/17 – 2020/21 (2016);
- The Agricultural Sector Development Programme Phase Two (ASDPII, 2016);
- The Climate Smart Agriculture Guideline (2017);

a supportive enabling environment.

1.2.1. Women and Gender Development Policy (2000)
The Ministry of Health, Community Development, Gender, Elderly and Children has facilitated the
formulation of the Women and Gender Development Policy in 2000 aiming to ensure that the gender
perspective is mainstreamed into all policies, programmes and strategies. Gender focal points were
established in other ministries, independent government departments, regional and local authorities.
These focal points were in turn responsible for gender mainstreaming in their respective plans and
programmes.
Thanks to this policy, Tanzania has seen improvements in education, health and water sectors, and in
the economic empowerment and participation in decision-making of women.
1.2.2. National Strategy for Gender Development (2008)
This policy goes further in reinforcing gender integration in national strategies, programmes and plans
than the Women and Gender Development Policy of 2000 by highlighting major issues and exposing
remaining challenges. It provides guidance on interventions to be made, identifies roles of various
actors and stakeholders, suggests co-ordination mechanisms to facilitate the participation of the
various actors and aims for the a more focused and more result-oriented implementation of gender
policies than the previous policy.
Major challenges remaining on the way to gender equality and equity come from the patriarchal
system customs and traditions, leading to the following needs:
- Empowering both women and men equally on the basis of merit;
- Ensuring that macro and micro policies are gender sensitive;
- Ensuring that the Legal Framework is gender sensitive;
- Strengthening institutional mechanisms for gender development;
- Ensuring sustainable partnerships and collaboration;
- Ensuring that adequate resources are available to address gender inequalities;
- Providing adequate capacities for coordination, monitoring, evaluation, advocacy and follow-
up of implementation of policies, strategies, plans and programmes.
Therefore, the objectives of this strategy are to:
- Speed up progress implementation of Women and Gender Development Policy;
- Speed up implementation of national, regional and international commitments on gender
equality and empowerment of women;
- Integrate gender issues into macro and sectoral policies;
- Link up with collaborating partners and civil organizations;
- Enhance coordination, accountability, monitoring, evaluation and research.
To that intent, twenty areas of gender concern have been identified, each subdivided into objectives
(medium and long term development goals), strategies (means to achieve objectives), activities (tasks
to implement strategies), performance based indicators (measures of activities outputs), actors
(responsible persons or institutions, with the ministry as lead institution), and a timeframe.

--- Table 1 on Page 117 ---
Climate change plans, programmes and strategies, especially through the National Strategy on
Gender and Climate Change (2013).

Several actors are designated to implement this strategy , under the coordination of the Ministry of
Health, Community Development, Gender, Elderly and Children, itself responsible for (i) coordinating
the implementation of the strategy and work closely with other actors, (ii) providing professional
backstopping to other implementers of gender development, (iii) reporting implementation progress,
(iv) collecting and disseminating information programmes and (v) maintaining National Gender
Database.
The Ministry relies on the support of:
- Other ministries, such as:
• Sector ministries, responsible for sector policy guidance, supervision, coordination
implementation and monitoring activities, and
• Central ministries responsible for providing general guidelines on gender
advancement and budgeting.
- National level institutions completed with regional and district level ones responsible for
capacity building and collection and dissemination of gender disaggregated data;
- The private sector supporting private actors’ action with roles and responsibilities to build local
capacity, empower communities, participate in monitoring and evaluation, mobilize
community resources and enhance community participation;
- Communities playing a role in planning, implementing and monitoring community activities;
- Finally, external support will be sought with development partners making use of existing
systems and processes to provide financial, technical and other support services.

1.3. Gender aspects in other national frameworks and programmes and in
agriculture
1.3.1. Tanzania Development Vision 2025 (1999)
The National Development Vision 2025 [9] is the guide for economic and social development efforts in
Tanzania. The objective of this Development Vision is to awaken, co-ordinate and direct the people's
efforts, minds and Tanzania national resources towards core sectors to enable the country to attain its
development goals and withstand the expected intensive economic competition.
This document was prepared with several stakeholders among which women have played a key role,
both as final beneficiaries of the strategy and as stakeholders to be involved in its implementation.
Vision 2025 aims for five overarching goals: (i) High Quality Livelihood, (ii) Peace, Stability and Unity,
(iii) Good Governance, (iv) A Well Educated and Learning Society and (v) A strong and Competitive
economy.
Overall, Tanzania aims for a good quality of life for all, to address social issues such as education, health
and the environment and to increase the involvement of people in working for their own development.
Those results should be achieved for both women and men, and girls and boys explicitly.
Nevertheless, it only outlines one gender specific objective under the “High quality livelihood” section:
“Gender equality and the empowerment of women in all socio-economic and political relations and
cultures”. It shows that gender balancing remains a cross-cutting goal even though more emphasis
could be made.

=== PAGE 121 ===
Page 118
1.3.2. Agriculture Climate Resilience Plan 2014 – 2019 (2014)
The Agriculture Climate Resilience Plan [10] provides an overview of the agriculture sector in Tanzania
through its main development trends and the expected impact of climate change by 2050. Key
observations concerning gender aspects are that smallholder farming is predominant in the country
with women more likely to be smallholders than men. This puts women at higher risk to climate
change. Better mainstreaming of gender aspects could have significant benefits for the uptake of
climate smart agricultural practices as women have been found to often more rapidly adopt
laborsaving climate smart agriculture practices.
This Plan provides recommendations of key investment to strengthen knowledge and systems to target
climate action through conducting a comprehensive assessment on gender and climate change issues
in the agriculture sector including:
- Climate change impacts on women and girls;
- Developing recommendations and guidelines for mainstreaming gender into Climate Change
and Adaptation related policies, strategies, programs, and budgets in respective areas of
jurisdiction;
- Identifying best practices in Tanzania and other countries;
- Identifying gender-appropriate technologies for activities related to water management,
climate-smart agriculture, and postharvest processing and value addition;
- Capacity building and awareness on climate change for women farmers;
- Recommendations for increasing women’s access to financial and productive resources.
The Plan also recommends developing a gender and agriculture coordination mechanism between the
Ministry of Agriculture Food Security and Cooperatives’ gender desk, gender committee and
Environment Management Unit. The Environment Management Unit would work with the gender desk
to mainstream gender in Climate Change and Adaptation in each stage of project, programme, policy
cycles. The gender committee would meet quarterly to evaluate progress for gender mainstreaming
in Climate Change and Adaptation related policies, strategies, programs and budgets.
1.3.3. National Five-Year Development Plan 2016/17 – 2020/21 (2016)
This development plan aims at “Nurturing Industrialization for Economic Transformation and Human
Development” [11]. It revolves around four priority action areas:
(i) Interventions for growth and industrialization;
(ii) Interventions for fostering human development and social transformation;
(iii) Interventions for improving environment for enterprise development;
(iv) Interventions for getting implementation right.
The strategy is further anchored on three pillars:
(i) Industrialization as a champion of economic and technological transformation;
(ii) Competitiveness as an active process to move from comparative advantage to competitive
advantage;
(iii) Regional integration and geography as the context for industrial development and
economic prosperity.
Overall, the gender dimension is only briefly described and little implementation activities or policy
changes are formulated. Still, the strategy highlights that interventions to ensure gender balance are

=== PAGE 122 ===
Page 119
needed to unleash women’s potentials to contribute to the envisaged social economic transformation
and more importantly as a matter of human rights. In addition, it sets enhancing gender equity through
affirmative action in accordance with international commitments and existing policy and legal
frameworks as a key objective.
Most relevant to gender aspects integration in agriculture and climate change actions are the aim to
reach gender income parity and to integrate Gender in the monitoring and evaluation process via sex-
disaggregated data.
Finally, despite limited operational objectives, the strategy dedicates two axes of intervention to
gender issues in:
- The social protection axis through “Women and Girls’ Empowerment Programme”:
participation in economic activities, child marriages and early pregnancies addressed under
this intervention;
- The agricultural axis through “Women and youth empowerment through agriculture”:
indicating, titling and surveying the agricultural land, linking farmers with financial institutions,
and improving the use of technology addressed under this intervention.
1.3.4. Agricultural Sector Development Programme Phase Two (ASDP II, 2016)
The Agricultural Sector Development Programme Phase Two [12] provides the general guidance for
agriculture development in Tanzania. It sets as development objective to transform the agricultural
sector (crops, livestock & fisheries) towards higher productivity, commercialization level and
smallholder farmer income for improved livelihood, food security and nutrition. Beneficiaries of this
programme include a variety of agriculture stakeholders with special attention to women and youth.
The women’s role is highlighted through the first cross-cutting objective: “Promoting gender equity
and ensuring that women and other vulnerable groups have equitable access to resources”. Indeed,
the Programme indicates that gender mainstreaming needs to be strengthened to increase the
benefits obtained from rural labor and enhance value addition. In particular, it states that agribusiness
investment policy needs to enable all groups to be involved at the high-value end of the market chain.
The programme also recommends that gender imbalances need to be addressed at all levels of the
institutional framework and sets several outcome indicators on gender mainstreaming:
- Participation of women in decision making and implementation of the solutions in agriculture;
- Equal benefits for women from programmes;
- Deduced disparities in the access to benefits of major programmes;
- Provision capacity building for gender empowerment;
- Targeting of participative support programmes for women.
1.3.5. Climate Smart Agriculture Guideline (2017)
The National Climate Smart Agriculture Programme [13] aims to accelerate the uptake of climate smart
agriculture in Tanzania to increase productivity and climate resilience in the agriculture sector (crops,
livestock and fisheries) and in the reduction of greenhouse gas emissions. It provides guidance for
adaptation and mitigation in building resilience of agricultural farming enterprises, improving and
increasing agricultural productivity and sustainability.
The guidance emphasizes on the mutual interactions between agriculture and climate change. Major
impacts of climate change on agriculture are the changes in rainfall for this rain fed agriculture, a

=== PAGE 123 ===
Page 120
general decline in agricultural productivity and in agro-diversity, the prevalence of crop pests and
diseases and the overall degradation of water and land resources. On the opposite, agriculture plays
an important role in climate change through deforestation and CO2 emissions due to land use change
and agriculture. In this context, Climate Smart Agriculture, defined as “Agriculture that sustainably
increases productivity, resilience (adaptation), reduces or removes Greenhouse Gases (GHGs)
(mitigation), and enhances achievement of national food security and development goals” appears
critical. It ensures sustainable agriculture, based upon integrated management of water, land and
ecosystems at landscape scale and includes techniques (mulching, intercropping, conservation
agriculture, pasture and manure management), and innovative practices, programmes, and policies,
(improved crop varieties, better weather forecasting, and risk insurance). It also emphasizes on agro-
ecological approaches to soil, nutrient, water and ecosystem management with explicit attention to
the importance of preserving genetic resources of crops and animals including wild relatives, critical in
developing resilience to shocks.
Given the norms and traditions in relation to the roles of women in agricultural production as well as
land tenure system, these technologies and practices face limitation when gender dimension is not
taken into consideration. The inadequacy of access for women to essential agriculture development
resources does not only increase hardships for women, but it places an extra burden on the entire
agriculture sector, the broader economy and society as a whole. Given the importance of gender in
Climate Smart Agriculture, a whole section is dedicated to its gender responsive approach, recognizing
the particular needs, priorities and realities of men and women for the design and implementation of
such actions.
The Gender responsive policy and practice recognize and address the specific needs and realities of
women and men, based on the social construction of gender roles. Gender transformative
interventions seek to transform gender roles and promote more gender-equitable relationships
between men and women. They challenge the underlying causes of gender inequality that is rooted in
broad political, economic, and socio-cultural structures. Because gender-transformative approaches
seek to change rigid gender roles and relations, such approaches often go beyond the individual level
to focus on interpersonal, social, structural, and institutional practices to address gender inequalities.
The guidance therefore formulated gender recommendations for each of the packages of technologies
and practices described in the guidance, through actions such as building women and girls capacities,
including gender considerations in the selection of solutions, proceeding to gender analysis of the
technologies and practices or ensuring women and girls can enhance their capacity to adapt to climate
change.
1.3.6. Gender in climate change plans, programmes and strategies
Tanzania, as a signatory of the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC)
developed several climate national plans, programmes and strategies, among which the First National
Communication (2003), National Adaptation Plan of Action (2007), Second National Communication
(2014) and Intended Nationally Determined Communication (2018).
Those major climate change plans, programmes and strategies all identified agriculture as a key sector
to fight climate change, and for which to increase resilience to climate change. Nevertheless, despite
gender aspects being overlooked in those documents, Tanzania developed a complementary
document, the National Strategy on Gender and Climate Change (2013), to integrate gender aspects
in its climate actions, especially in agriculture.

=== PAGE 124 ===
Page 121
1.3.6.1. First National Communication (2003)
Tanzania designed its First National Communication (NC1) including technological and policy options
for mitigation of greenhouse gas emissions as well as assessment of vulnerability and adaptation to
climate change impacts. Tanzania assessed its greenhouse gas emissions and set its mitigation targets
in energy, industry, transport, forestry, land use and agriculture. The vulnerability analysis and
adaptation options covered water resources, crop production, grassland and livestock, coastal
resources and structures, forests, wildlife and biodiversity and health. Therefore, agriculture was a key
component of the national climate strategies both in mitigation and adaptation. Nevertheless, gender
aspects were overlooked in this communication, mostly being used for some background presentation
[14].
1.3.6.2. National Adaptation Programme of Action (2007)
Tanzania developed its National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA) to enhance its ability to deal
with climate change and to assess its climate change related vulnerabilities in various economic
sectors. The NAPA preparation process involved looking at the effects of climate change as a threat
mainly to the agrarian population that still depends on subsistence agriculture for their daily livelihood.
Tanzania identified vulnerabilities in agriculture, water, health, forestry, wetlands, energy, coastal and
marine resources, wildlife, tourism, and industry. It has set agriculture, livestock, forestry, water,
coastal and marine resources, health, wildlife, industry, energy, wetlands, human settlements, tourism
and land use as adaptation priority sectors, and prioritizes project activities in those sectors.
Agriculture was highlighted as key sector to adapt to climate change. The only reference to gender
aspects other than contextual elements is found in the “Climate change adaptation through
participatory reforestation in Kilimanjaro mountain” project that aims to build stakeholders’ (among
which women) capacities on climate change adaptation and biodiversity conservation [15].
1.3.6.3. Second National Communication (2014)
The Second National Communication (NC2) further develops Tanzania climate overview, assessment
and planning, and builds on the learnings derived from NC1 implementation. Mitigation actions
focused on energy, waste management, transport, industry, agriculture, land use and forestry sectors.
Vulnerability assessments and adaptation measures were elaborated for water, health, agriculture,
livestock, forestry, wildlife, tourism, coastal and marine environments. Agriculture was identified as a
major sector for both climate mitigation and adaptation. Gender aspects were also overlooked in this
document, mostly being used for some background presentation [16].
1.3.6.4. Nationally Determined Communication (2018)
Tanzania designed its Nationally Determined Communication (NDC) to provide a concise and
structured presentation of its climate strategy to enhance adaptive capacity to climate change,
increase resilience of ecosystems and enhance the participation in climate change mitigation activities
to contribute to international efforts while ensuring sustainable development. Mitigation options were
developed for energy, transport, waste management, and forest sectors, while adaptation options
were set for agriculture, livestock, forestry, energy, coastal marine environment, fisheries, water
resources, tourism, human settlements and health sectors. Similarly to the National Communications
and to the National Adaptation Plan of Action, agriculture is at the center of the strategy, however
gender aspects are not mentioned at all this time [17].

=== PAGE 125 ===
Page 122
1.3.6.5. National Strategy on Gender and Climate Change (2013)
The National Strategy on Gender and Climate Change [18] outlines six thematic areas (agriculture,
water, health, energy, forests and integrated coastal management), addresses barriers faced by
women as agents for change, and identifies cross-cutting dimensions such as capacity building,
awareness and policy
The strategy is expected to guide national efforts for addressing the impacts of climate change on
gender inequalities as women suffer from historic disadvantages: their limited access and control over
decision-making, environmental and economic resources and their restricted rights. Indeed, gender
inequality can worsen the impacts of climate change while, taking steps to narrow the gender gap and
empower women can also reduce these impacts. Climate change and gender inequality are therefore
interlinked. By exacerbating inequality overall, climate change slows down progress towards gender
equality and henceforth impedes efforts to achieve wider goals such as poverty reduction and
sustainable development.
The disproportionate burden of climate change on women can be countered by their empowerment
and recognition. Women are powerful agents of change and their leadership is critical. Women can
enhance strategies related to integrated coastal management, forest, agriculture, water, health and
energy, for example. Incorporating a gender perspective successfully and effectively requires that men
and women understand the process of climate change and share information on counteracting its
negative impacts on an equal basis to both women and men. It is important that women have equal
access to knowledge, awareness, capacity building, resources and technology, which are prerequisites
in influencing climate change. Likewise, it is fundamental that women participate more actively in
decision-making and policy development at all levels. To this end, gender equality and women
empowerment underlie this strategy, as two globally recognized complementary principles.
For each priority sector, a situation analysis and a gender assessment are presented with examples of
actions, further declined in objectives and the related action steps, indicators of success and
responsible stakeholders. For the agriculture sector, seven objectives are set:
(i) Building and strengthening institutional understanding on gender, climate change and
agriculture;
(ii) Improving agriculture related infrastructures to adapt to the effects of climate change;
(iii) Enhancing local community participation of women and men in components of agricultural
sector activities;
(iv) Land tenure and customary law to incorporate women’s access and title to land;
(v) Strengthening early warning system in agriculture to facilitate weather forecasting;
(vi) Ensuring gender-responsive budget, and;
(vii) Enhancing participation of women and girl through formal and informal education in the
agricultural sector.
In addition, seven cross-cutting objectives have been set for capacity building, awareness and policy
change:
(i) Ensuring that gender responsive climate change criteria are fully integrated in relevant
national sector policies;
(ii) Enhancing the mainstreaming of gender and climate change in the implementation of
national development plans;
(iii) Developing a sustainable financing mechanism for climate change challenges including
gender projects;

=== PAGE 126 ===
Page 123
(iv) Strengthening the institutional framework for climate change governance and improved
coordination at all levels;
(v) Promoting legal and regulatory framework for gender and climate change with specific
emphasis in empowerment of women for accessing and controlling resources;
(vi) Building the institutional capacity to improve the understanding and supporting process
that mainstream gender and climate change in relevant government sectors, and;
(vii) Increasing women and men awareness on climate change and its impact on relevant
sectors.

=== PAGE 127 ===
Page 124
2. Gender overview, barriers and challenges in Tanzania
2.1. General gender overview, barriers and challenges in Tanzania
2.1.1. Overview
Women in Tanzania suffer from inequalities in most of the aspects of their lives, especially regarding
income generation, years of schooling and access to labor, as shown below [19]:

The Human Development Index (HDI) is a summary measure of achievements in three key dimensions
of human development: a long and healthy life, access to knowledge and a decent standard of living.
As shown above, in addition to Tanzania lagging behind other countries (HDI of 0,737 worldwide),
women are particularly at risk. The gender gap has slightly narrowed over the years, but has remained
relatively constant since 2014, showing little improvement in women’s situation compared to men in
Tanzania despite several measures implemented by the government.
