Female to male ratio has always remained higher than 1, and even higher than 1.5 since 2011, showing
a much larger likelihood for women to be unemployed than men. This can be further illustrated with
the evolution of labor force participations. Both men and women tend to be more at risk of
unemployment, as the participation rate decreased almost every year, for both men and women (only
exception being women in 2005). The decrease in participation rate has been stronger for women than
men on average, once again showing that women are more at risk of being unemployed than men.
Those observations are further explained and completed in the following sections.
2.1.2. Barriers and challenges
Gender inequalities are the major factors that have increased women’s vulnerability to climate change,
compared to men. Although governments in developing countries have endeavored to formulate
gender-responsive budgets, their implementation has been constrained by (i) lack of technical capacity
in gender, (ii) lack of skills and expertise in planning, monitoring, and evaluation of budgets among
those hired to implement Gender Responsive Budget initiatives, (iii) limited or no resources allocated
to gender activities, (iv) inadequate research and gender-disaggregated data, (v) negative cultural
practices, and (vi) presence of gender-blind sectoral policies especially those that influence local
development initiatives.
For Tanzania, annual budget guidelines are often vague, actor roles are not clear and more generally,
the importance of gender is downplayed by placing it among cross-cutting issues. Results show that
most national level policies in Tanzania recognize the need for gender equality by specifying issues of
concern, but fall short of reflecting clear strategies, actions, or means of addressing the issues
highlighted [20].
This situation leads to gender unbalancing towards women. Indeed, the Gender Development Index is
0.948 (GDI, measure of gender inequalities in three dimensions: health, education and command over
economic resources) and the Gender Inequalities Index is 0.556 (GII, gender-based disadvantage in
three dimensions: reproductive health, empowerment and the labor market) [19], [21]. Tanzania
belongs to Gender Development Index group 3 out of 5 (group 5 being the countries with highest
unbalance) and is ranked 140 for the Gender Inequalities Index. Both indexes show Tanzania has to
increase its efforts to reduce gender unbalance and provide similar opportunities to women.
To identify main barriers to gender equality and equity, Tanzania has developed a Country Gender
Profile (2016) [7], going through an in-depth assessment of eight priority sectors for the development
of gender balance. Synthetic presentations of the main observations are:
Priority Sector Main observations
Economy Despite a decline of women’s poverty incidence over the last years from 34.4% in
2007 down to 28.2% in 2012, women remain more struck by poverty than men
and their financial safety is more at risk. Women are the main workforce in
agriculture, an economic sector that is less dynamic than the overall economy
thus generating an intrinsic delay in women’s economic development. Overall,
while labor laws prohibit any kind of discrimination, the proportion of
unemployed women remains higher, youth unemployment rises for girls, women
are more likely to have vulnerable and low paying jobs, and less likely to own
enterprises than men. Challenges arise from inadequate gender mainstreaming
in the economy, especially due to inadequate policy, legislations and support
systems, as well as planning budgeting and monitoring through gender

disaggregated data. Therefore, Tanzania will need to set improved sectorial
economic frameworks, and develop strategies to mainstream and monitor
gender balancing improvement.
Healthcare The number of health facilities is rising, women have better access to health
insurance funds (National Health Insurance Fund and Community Health Fund)
and better follow-up of pregnancy/birth, reducing maternal mortality rates and
early child deaths. Still, food insecurity and HIV prevalence (6.2% for women
against 3.8% for men) are higher for women than for men. The main challenges
are the low budgeting, general structural issues faced by women (persistent
cultural issues, poverty, inadequate information and access to care, cultural
biases preventing women from diagnosis). Women also lack of a comprehensive
programme for reproductive and child health. Tanzania will need to develop
comprehensive health gender assessments and increase national budgeting for
healthcare. It will also need to reinforce awareness especially on HIV/AIDS and
other epidemies and invest in outreach services in regions and districts.
Education The girls-to-boys ratio is relatively balanced at pre-primary and primary school
but there is a much lower rate of girls to boys at secondary and higher education
levels. More specifically, there is a low representation of women in Science,
Technology, Engineering and Mathematics (STEM) subjects. While literacy is
improving, female literacy still lags behind that of males. Main challenges for
female education remain the low retention and achievement rates leading to low
catchment at secondary and tertiary levels, and the low enrollment of girls in
STEM subjects. Policies and plans need to support an increase in the retention
and achievement rates, enhance the willingness of local communities to invest in
girls’ education, and provide a more enabling school environment to girls (e.g.
improved gender pedagogy, special incentives for female teachers to set them as
role models, support for female empowerment).
Politics Women have achieved an improved political representation, especially in the
Parliament with Tanzania being one of the countries with the highest share of
women in its Parliament (37% in 2015) thanks to temporary special measures
targeting 50-50 gender representation. Furthermore, several public entities are
headed by women as are some key political positions within the central
government. However, women are still under-represented in most of the political
bodies and at their top especially at local level where the decision-making process
remains male dominated. Challenges faced by women in Tanzania are mainly due
to the patriarchal ideology limiting women representation in politics, and to
inadequate awareness and enforcement of the 2010 Election Act, that constrains
women from enjoying the right to make a claim in the incidence of a violation
during the electoral process. Tanzania will need to institutionalize gender
sensitive voter education and civic education in grassroots leadership structures.
Violence and An increasing number of women are breaking down gender-based violence
abuse silence but this issue still remains highly prevalent, especially through genital
mutilation (14.6% of women), physical (1/3 of women) and sexual violence (1/10
women has experienced sexual intercourse against their will). Tanzania only
provides limited law enforcement and legal protection to women, slowing down
the removal of issues such as early marriage. Main challenges to be addressed by
Tanzania concern the inability for women to challenge harmful traditions and
shortcomings in enforcement of the law; and to rely on adequate resources.
Furthermore, several contradictions exist between different laws and cultural or
religious frameworks (especially the 1971 Marriage Act), and often times
customary and religious laws prevent women from accessing their legal rights

=== PAGE 131 ===
Page 128
(access to and ownership and use of land among others). To solve those issues,
the country needs to improve coordination between the police, health facility,
and judiciary workers. It will need to also provide facilities such as shelters and
safe houses for victims, as well as medical treatment and counselling.
Rights and As a signatory of the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination
legal against Women, Tanzania is accelerating the integration of gender aspects in its
framework Constitution and at all levels. There is an increase in the number of women
accessing courts of laws, but a lot of work remains to be done to remove barriers
for women arising from statutory, customary and religious laws. Despite the 2014
updating of the Constitution further advancing gender equality, several barriers
remain to develop an adequate legal framework for women such as the low legal
literacy of populations and the slow legal updating processes (marriage, property,
inheritance, etc.). Also, the lack of gender disaggregated data does not allow to
monitor changes efficiently, while gender equality frameworks are not enough
used to inform policy, practice and planning. Tanzania will therefore need to
develop customized policies, mechanisms and procedures for collecting,
reporting and monitoring gender in legal sector institutions as early as possible.
Climate change Structures and policies are being set up to spearhead interventions and
coordinate activities related to climate change and gender integration (National
Climate Change Strategy and Reducing Emissions from Deforestation and Forest
Degradation - REDD - strategy among others). Those structures and policies try to
mainstream gender in climate change, but women’s response to climate change
remains limited to informal, small level actions due to their limited capacity,
especially financial capacity. Challenges to further integrate women in climate
change actions include women’s poverty and the inequalities in decision. There is
also a lack of a comprehensive database on women’s and men’s traditional
knowledge and mechanisms for addressing the impacts of environmental and
climate change, limiting the integration of traditional knowledge in climate
change response. Tanzania needs to align climate actions with the three main
frameworks driving gender equality CEDAW, Beijing Platform for Action and
Sustainable Development Goals, each of those frameworks having already been
ratified by the country.
Information Women suffer from a lower access to communication media and tools and a
and lower representation on media, with the male image still being depicted more
communication positively than that of women. Additionally, the weak enforcement of laws and
pressures for profit maximization have given room for negative portrayals of
women. Major challenges are the inadequate integration of gender in the
implementation of information and communication related policies, the absence
of gender policy to guide for instance the editorial practices of the media, and the
unregulated distribution and dissemination of abusive information about women.
The country needs to develop linkages between media and gender-oriented
policies and to develop sex-disaggregated statistics.
2.2. Focus on gender overview, barriers and challenges in the agriculture sector in
Tanzania
Women in Tanzania working in the agriculture sector experience lower agricultural productivity
(defined as the monetary value of output per unit of land) and higher vulnerability than men for four
main reasons: (i) land ownership barriers, (ii) labor access unbalancing, (iii) technical and technological
barriers, (iv) financial barriers.

In addition, gender differentiated farm activities suggest that women and men have different
experience and knowledge of the natural environment. Differences also exist with regard to owning
and controlling resources, in particular land. As a consequence, men are more likely than women to
be able to adapt to risks emerging from climate variability and the associated natural disasters [8].
Empowering women in agriculture appears to be of critical concern to fight climate change and adapt
agriculture to its adverse effects, all the more so as low agricultural productivity tends to reduce yields-
per-hectare and leads to more intense farming—resulting in overcultivation, soil erosion, and land
degradation [22].
The Integrated Labour Force Survey (ILFS, 2014) – Analytical report [23] provides preliminary general
insights on barriers faced by women in accessing jobs and more specifically in agriculture. Below is a
selection of interesting data from 2014 and the first insights they provide:

unbalance unemployment brings, it is to be noted that while women tend to be unemployed because
they have to handle housework, men tend to attend school when not employed. This major difference
further increases the unbalancing: prolonged school attendance leads to potential more attractive

professional opportunities for men, while housework doesn’t generate any income for women.
Furthermore, when employed, women are more likely than men to be unpaid when working in farms
or not to own the farms in which they work. Agriculture holds a large share of Tanzania economy
(26.7% of the GDP [24]) and provides employment to the majority of the country, therefore the
unbalance severely hinders women’s economic development.
Vulnerabilities: When employed, women again have to face several challenges in addition to financial
ones. A very high percentage of them are vulnerable workers, and they tend to suffer from work
related injuries and illnesses more than men, especially in agriculture and related sectors.
Female headed households and housework: Women spend more time than men on housework, while
men spend more time on income generating activities further increasing economic unbalancing.
Women are also more likely to be widowed or divorced than men, leading to female headed
households (25.4% in 2017 [25]). As such, women are more likely to only rely on a single income
provider, contrary to male headed households where men can rely on women to provide income as
well. Indeed, on average, women headed households count 3.9 people against 4.9 for male headed
households [26]. This situation further puts women in risky financial positions.
Economic differences: Finally, women earn lower incomes than men across all sectors. Women
working in agriculture face a critical financial situation. Despite a slightly lower relative difference with
men, compared to the difference across all sectors, incomes in agriculture are much lower than in
other sectors, making women working in agriculture the lowest earning population.
Overall, the gender gap in agriculture is estimated to have an annual cost of $105 million (0.46% of
GDP. This highlights the large impact both unconditional (strict difference in the value of output per
hectare between male and female) and conditional (further comparison taking into account that
women tend to hold smaller plots than men, increasing the unbalance and the vulnerability to agro-
climatic conditions) gender balancing solutions could bring, with respectively an expected increase of
agricultural productivity of 16% or 30%. In addition, reducing the gender gap could increase annual
crop output by 2.1%, lift 80,000 people out of poverty and reduce the incidence of undernourishment
by 0.7% as women tend to spend more of their income on children’s health and education [22].
Below is a focus on the four main barriers highlighted at the beginning of the section [8], [27].
2.2.1. Land ownership barriers
Due to legal frameworks, customary and traditional land ownership norms, Tanzanian women face
several land ownership barriers. They hold only few and small plots, often of lower quality and
productivity and have limited room to enjoy the benefits of land they own.
Tanzania’s land reforms, such as the Land Act and the Village Land Act of 1999, have the aim of
eradicating gender inequalities in land ownership. Specifically, Section 3 (2) of the Village Land Act
stipulates: “the right of every adult woman to acquire, hold, use, deal with and transmit land by or
obtain land through the operations of a will, shall be to the same extent and subject to the same
restrictions as the right of every adult man” [28]. Legally, women may hold, own and dispose of
property and have the same land rights as men. Indeed, laws provide for strong protections of women
landowners, recognize a wife‘s right to household land upon widowhood or divorce, prevent village
land councils from discriminating against women, and allocate to women a certain number of seats on
the councils, which administer occupancy rights and adjudicate land disputes in rural areas.

=== PAGE 134 ===
Page 131
Despite legal protection and reforms, customary land tenure norms, vesting control of property in men
continue to influence decisions and practices in rural areas while women‘s rights to land is reduced to
their roles as wives or daughters. By some estimates, 82% of land in Tanzania is administered under
customary land tenure. Women have usufruct rights through spouses or male family members but
cannot normally own or transfer land. Upon marriage, a couple is granted land, usually by the man‘s
family. Of that land, the man allocates small garden plots to his wife so she can cultivate food crops to
provide for household needs, which is a primary responsibility of women. However, this does not give
women secure property rights.
This situation translates into 73% of landholders being men, leaving only 27% of women landholders.
As owners, women tend to have smaller and fewer plots, average land of 0.6 hectares against 1
hectare, and to own less livestock than men. Women usually manage more fragile lands and their plots
are more likely to be affected by floods, landslides and the resulting soil degradation, against which
they cannot afford fertilizers due to limited income, and erosion. Women’s plots are largely rain-fed
and use less hired labor. While most of the plots are irrigated by flooding (71%) and buckets (18%),
considerable gender differences exist with regards to irrigation. Additionally, female landholders are
older on average than their male counterparts. 27% of male holders are aged 25 - 34 years against 19%
for female, and 25% of female holders are older than 55 against 15% for male. It suggests that women
are likely to access land at a later stage of their (productive) life. Finally, women cannot use the land
as collateral as their husband can sell it at any time. If they become widowed or divorced, their
husband‘s clan may take possession of the land as well as the rest of the household‘s material goods.
2.2.2. Labor access unbalancing
Women in Tanzania face several labor access constraints. They almost single handedly handle
housework and provide cash necessary for household needs. They are also often reduced to unpaid or
low paid productive activities and cannot rely on male labor the same way male land holders rely on
female productive labor.
Women's ability to work on their plots is constrained by social expectations as they perform significant
quantities of unpaid care and domestic work. Women are also expected to provide the cash necessary
to meet household maintenance needs and when cash is short this requires engaging in off-farm casual
waged labor or petty trading. Overall, this limits women's flexibility in their choices by reducing labor
availability for on-farm and off-farm work, reducing their opportunities to develop time consuming
climate-smart agricultural techniques and impacting cash and non-cash resources women are able to
generate. This may contribute to the adoption of unsustainable agricultural practices and increase
vulnerability to climatic variation.
Women often contribute unpaid labor on their husband's plots of land or are expected to work on it
before being able to work on their own plots. Women do not control the output of the work they
proceed for their husbands. Such labor forced by their husbands often comes hand in hand with
gender-based violence. Men often use gender-based violence to enforce labor allocation decisions
regarding the use of their wives' labor on their plots and to ensure the performance of unpaid care
and domestic work by women, and especially food preparation for men.
Finally, and of high impact, women farmers use less hired labor and are less able to access adequate
amounts of male family labor to work on their plots, affecting the total amount of labor used in farming
for female landholders. Overall, the inability to acquire adequate amounts of male family labor
generates 97.3% of the gender gap in agricultural productivity in Tanzania, around US$102 million. On
the opposite, women are hired more often as casual laborers than men and their mean wages are

=== PAGE 135 ===
Page 132
almost three times lower than those of men in agriculture. This might have consequences in terms of
productivity and profitability of women farming activities and of the time-burden overload as both
male and female farmers tend to use more female than male casual labor.
2.2.3. Technical and technological barriers
Women in Tanzania frequently have unequal access to key agricultural inputs such as land, labor,
knowledge such as the access to irrigation, pesticides, fertilizers and implements, and improved seeds
and crops.
92% of plots held by women are irrigated by flooding against 8% by bucket, as opposed to respectively
63% and 23% of men’s plots. Very few women benefit from mechanical irrigation systems such as
sprinklers, drip irrigation or water hoses for their own plots. Women use lower levels of advanced
agricultural technologies, notably pesticides, fertilizer and implements. Lower use of pesticides and
inorganic fertilizer by women is equivalent to potential gross gains in GDP of over $19.3 million. Their
access to agricultural implements is also significantly lower than that of men, explaining 8% of the
gender gap in agricultural productivity. Bringing lesser use of pesticides and inorganic fertilizer along
with poor quality tools and implements together also contribute to the gender gap in agricultural
productivity.
Where farmers do use improved seeds, men are more likely than women to use improved seeds,
creating a gender gap. Women farmers are particularly affected by the inadequate number of
agricultural extension service officers, which results in a lack of quality technical advice at the village
and household level. Access to productive resources such as land, modern inputs, technology,
education and financial services is a critical determinant to increase women’s agricultural productivity.
2.2.4. Financial barriers
Women in Tanzania face several financial barriers as they make lower incomes than men, tend to grow
food crops (also called priority crops) while men grow cash crops (also called strategic crops), and have
limited access to financial solutions such as credit, loans or guarantees.
Farm activities are the most important source of income for rural households and account for
approximately half of household incomes across all expenditure quintiles. Still, self-employed women
in agriculture earn significantly less than men, although there are significant regional variations. They
are more likely to be employed as casual farm labor and paid three times less than men for such work.
Despite their work on jointly operated plots being greater than that of their husbands, husbands
control which crops are produced on the jointly operated plot, decide when production is surplus to
household requirements, when and where crops will be disposed of, and retain control of the cash
generated.
There is also some evidence that women are more likely than men to spend earned income on their
family's needs (pay for clothing, health care, school fees and such as expenses for household
maintenance are considered to be the responsibility of a wife), while money earned by husbands is not
shared with their wives as they control the money and decide how to use it. Furthermore, men use
gender-based violence to take control of cash and non-cash resources generated by women, including
loans that women take from village savings and loan groups.
Men in Tanzania are largely responsible for cash crop farming and income-generating activities while
women take charge of food crops, help with cash crops, and provide the bulk of unpaid labor for

=== PAGE 136 ===
Page 133
household duties. Self-employed women in agriculture are more likely to use their land for subsistence
farming than for commercial farming. Due to unequal bargaining power within households, men are
more likely to influence the coping and adaptation strategies adopted within male-headed households
therefore favoring cash crops over food crops, increasing risks related to food security.
Few women can access credit and loans from banks and financial institutions as they lack formal
property rights necessary as collateral. Microcredit, though available, is limited and only 6% of
Tanzanians borrow from microcredit institutions. Group lending is an established practice in Tanzania,
but effective individual lending that would allow small enterprises to expand is underdeveloped
because of banking regulations.

=== PAGE 137 ===
Page 134
3. Actions and projects to reduce gender unbalance in Tanzania
3.1. CRDB Bank gender actions and policies
CRDB Bank is committed to fighting gender inequalities and inequities in Tanzania. To that intent, it
has developed its own Gender Policy, aligned with Tanzania’s gender commitments. By means of
implementation, CRDB has designed ad-hoc women-oriented products and solutions, such as the
Malkia Account.
3.1.1. CRDB Gender Policy
CRDB Bank has developed a Gender Policy in 2019 [29], based on the premise that Tanzania as a
country recognizes gender inequality as a major obstacle to socio-economic development of its people.
This policy outlines all gender-related aspects of CRDB Bank’s activities, defining gender as “social
attributes and opportunities associated with being male and female and the relationships between
women and men and girls and boys, as well as the relations between women and those between men.
These attributes, opportunities and relationships are socially constructed and are learned through
socialization processes.”
Internally, the policy implementation is under the responsibility of the Board of Directors and the
Senior Management and Directors’ (especially that of the Director of Risk and Compliance, and of the
Director of Human Resources), with support from Internal Audit and Lending Units teams. The policy
is applicable to all CRDB Bank’s staff, business units, departments, branches and subsidiaries, and to
other CRDB stakeholders (suppliers, implementing partners such as Tanzanian public and/or private
sector institution/firm/company or individuals).
CRDB Bank highlights that gender equality is both a development goal in itself and a precondition for
the achievement of other development outcomes. The bank has taken and continues to take various
measures to ensure gender equality and gender equity among all its employees and customers which
aims at achieving full and equal participation between women and men. The policy, aligned with CRDB
Bank’s internal policies and international and national agreements and policies applicable to Tanzania
and CRDB Bank, reaffirms CRDB Bank’s commitment to:
(i) Reduce gender disparities in leadership positions;
(ii) Transform gender relations;
(iii) Promote women’s empowerment;
(iv) Strengthen organisational structures and systems for gender equality by incorporating into
policies, plans, strategies and programmes;
(v) Redress gender gaps and inequalities between men and women;
(vi) Guide different bank’s business units on gender issues.
Most relevant to CRDB Bank’s action in climate and agriculture, the bank reminds that resource
shortages are gendered (worsening shortage and lack of access to water, energy and food sources, the
latter being highly gendered) and that climate change is hitting women and girls harder (women and
men do not experience the impacts of climate change and environmental degradation equally).
Women, will be more likely carrying additional work from unpredictable climate and worsening crop
failures. Women’s lack of access to land, finance and other resources increases their vulnerability to
climate change that acts as a magnifying glass (increased risks of gender inequalities in the access to
and control of resources and decision making power, of women poverty, of undermining women’s

=== PAGE 138 ===
Page 135
adaptive capabilities, and of preventing them from participating equally in solutions to climate
change).
To address climate change and agriculture challenges, CRDB Bank has adopted several commitments
and principles. The bank actively promotes gender issue within and outside the bank through the
delivery of financial services including loans and project/programs management. The bank
mainstreams gender in projects at all stages from identification to post evaluation. Indeed, CRDB
addresses gender considerations as part of the initial social assessment for all of its projects, including
program and sector loans. It is a pre-requisite for appraising and decision making. In addition, special
design features and strategies are built into projects to facilitate and encourage women’s involvement
and ensure tangible benefits to women. In implementing the provisions of this policy, the bank follows
six gender principles:
(i) Know them - Understand socio-economic and cultural contexts, gender norms and
perceptions;
(ii) Understand the needs and aspirations of women, men, boys and girls;
(iii) Understand the opportunities, and know where the levers for solutions lie;
(iv) Design with them - Develop appropriate, contextualised solutions and projects to ensure
all interventions are gender aware and could transform gender relations;
(v) Level the field - Use social and behaviour change communication and evidence-based
advocacy at household, community, and public policy levels to influence gender norms,
perceptions, and policies that perpetuate exclusion; and
(vi) Be accountable to them - Implement with clear roadmap for change, account for results
on sector, gender and generational outcomes, monitor progress and after-effects.
3.1.2. Malkia Account
CRDB Bank has created a bank account solution, the Malkia Account, tailored to women’s needs [30].
The Malkia Account is designed to help women meet their financial obligations and personal goals,
including in investment, education, business and healthcare by:
- Easing loaning conditions;
- Providing attractive loans: annual lending interest rate of 14% against 24% before Malkia
Accounts creation;
- Providing attractive interest rates on deposits: from 1.5% to 2.5%, depending on the amount
deposited;
- Providing special insurance products with discount [31].
CRDB Bank has also come up with a new scheme for women to access loans without collateral [32].
Overall, Malkia Account aims to help women boost their business. In addition to specific financial
products, women can also benefit from counseling from “Malkia Rafiki”, women experts providing
business advice and education on how to start their business [33]. More than 30,000 women have
already opened Malkia accounts showing its success [34].

=== PAGE 139 ===
Page 136
3.2. Other projects in agriculture
3.2.1 Policy Action for Climate Change Adaptation in East Africa
The “Policy Action for Climate Change Adaptation”, led by Climate Change, Agriculture, and Food
Security between 2014 and 2020, is a project seeking to inform and link policies and institutions from
national to local level for the development and adoption of climate-resilient food systems in Uganda
and Tanzania. Scientific communities and policy actors analyzed policy formulation and
implementation processes, trade-off analysis, future socio-economic and climate scenario
development, creating evidence-based gender awareness, and applied information economics to
better inform the implementation of climate-resilient policies, especially to integrate gender-
responsive climate-smart agricultural practices across multiple scales in the two countries (increased
appreciation of and actions towards including gender-sensitive policies and implementation).
Evidence was generated showing gender mainstreaming gaps in policy documents at national and sub-
national levels and gaps in gender budgeting at local government level. Almost half of the documents
reviewed in this project acknowledged the role of women as major actors in agriculture, but women
were portrayed as marginalized and vulnerable, with a disconnection in gender integration between
the national and district and lower levels of governance [35], [36].
3.2.2 Land tenure projects
Land tenure assistance projects, such as “Strengthening Women’s Land Tenure Rights in Matembo”,
led by the United States Agency for International Development (USAID) and DAI between 2015 and
2021, aim support women with land tenure issues by creating customary certificates of occupancy for
landholders to document land rights. Indeed, those rights are often denied to women as they are not
aware about them. Those certifications give more incentive to women to participate in agricultural
activities. Such ownerships spell greater security and independence for women, reduced conflict and
better use of resources for communities, and greater investment and agricultural productivity at
national scale. Thanks to those rights, women are granted equal stakes in couples’ properties and can
focus more on agricultural activities [37], [38], [39].
3.3 Other actions and projects to reduce gender unbalance
Women face several vulnerabilities in several sectors. Among other sectors where actions are
currently, or have recently been, implemented by international and local actors are:
- Education: projects such as “Waache Wasome - Let them Learn” led by the USAID and the
Bantwana Wolrd Education Initiative between 2016 and 2021, support highly vulnerable girls
who dropped out of school, to create a friendlier and more supportive environment for girls
at school and to provide alternative education pathways. Many girls face several barriers to
finishing their education among which poverty of their parents leading to investing more into
boys education, or gender-based violence, early marriage, pregnancy, and physical, sexual,
and emotional violence as estimated 44% of adolescent girls in Tanzania have given birth or
are pregnant before age 19, while by age 17, an estimated 58% of girls who are married have
dropped out [40], [41];
- Access to legal support: projects such as the common project led by USAID and Women in Law
and Development in Africa provide legal aid and gender-based violence services [42];

- Access to water and sanitation: projects such as “Water Resources Integration Development
Initiative – WARIDI” led by the USAID and Tetra Tech between 2016 and 2021, improve access
to sanitation and hygiene [47], [48];
- Financial security: projects such as “Women’s Empowerment and Entrepreneurship
Initiative” led by the USAID, Dining for Women and African People & Wildlife between 2016
and 2017, develop economic activities, such as beehive entrepreneurship, to provide financial
independence to women and allow them to gain status and decision-making power above the
traditional household roles limitations. Several projects also aimed to train groups of
vulnerable people on business skills, financial management and gender issues, like effective
communications skills, contraceptive methods and gender-based violence to help women
take control of their finances and raise up their communities, and understand their health
risks and opportunities to build bright futures [49], [50], [51].

--- Table 1 on Page 140 ---
Reinforcement of the participation in the political life: projects such as “Wanawake
Wanaweza” led by the USAID and the UN Fund for Women between 2018 and 2022, increase
female participation in political parties and electoral processes. Several projects aimed to
counter closing civic and political space, bolster freedom of expression, improve the advocacy
and communication capabilities of local organizations, promote transparent and accountable
systems free from corruption, and strengthen human rights among vulnerable populations,
especially by increasing the diversity of both candidates and internal party decision-makers
[42], [43], [44];
Access to health and protection against HIV: projects such as “Sauti” led by the USAID
between 2015 and 2020, raise awareness and create demand for medical services, to increase
family planning and HIV testing and counselling. Such projects especially focused on
vulnerable populations as nearly one in three girls experiences some form of sexual violence
before turning 18, while almost four in 10 women have experienced physical violence, and
one in five women over age 15 reports experiencing sexual violence in her lifetime. Therefore,
the UNAIDS 90-90-90 goals intended to let 90% of people infected with HIV to be aware of it,
90% of people diagnosed receiving sustained antiretroviral therapy, and 90 of people under
retroviral therapy to have viral suppression [45], [46];
Access to water and sanitation: projects such as “Water Resources Integration Development
Initiative – WARIDI” led by the USAID and Tetra Tech between 2016 and 2021, improve access
to sanitation and hygiene [47], [48];

=== PAGE 141 ===
Page 138
4. Problem tree analysis
Below is the problem tree analysis realized for the “Tanzania Agriculture Climate Adaptation Technology Deployment Programme” project:

=== PAGE 142 ===
Page 139
The overall objective of the TACATDP is to support farmers with adaptation tools, knowledge, and
capacity to enable best adaptation options to current and future climatic contexts and agronomic
specificities. It will therefore intend to address several of the barriers and challenges specifically
identified for women, either directly or indirectly through the following major components:
- Adaptation credits:
(i) Promotion of long-term investment projects in agriculture to create a reliable market
for agricultural products, enhance food security and encourage climate resilient and
environmental ethical practices;
(ii) Increase of the financial inclusion of smallholders farmers and increasing their
productivity by making high cost technologies accessible and establishing service
delivery channels easing the access to financial services;
(iii) Introduction of simplified and affordable financial products with potential
considerations to insure crops;
(iv) Promotion of climate resilient agriculture to encourage organic farming practice and
reduce the utilization of uncertified seeds, industrial fertilizers and pesticides, and the
improper usage of water resources and labor intensity;
(v) Aggregation of smallholder farmers to intensify farming activities through large scale
environmental friendly infrastructures;
(vi) Support to the structuring of smallholders farming around climate resilient agriculture
practices, as well as additional support to promote saving accounts, health insurance
etc.
Output (i) will reinforce women’s economic integration and income generation by structuring the
market and providing support for food safety instead of food safety almost entirely relying on women
(barrier 2.2.4). Outputs (ii), (v) and (vi) will directly influence women as most of them are smallholders,
reducing their vulnerability and structuring their activity in larger plots (barrier 2.2.1). Outputs (iii) and
(iv) will address the unequal access to technologies, techniques and financial solutions, leveling the
field between men and women (barrier 2.2.3).
- Guarantee support: Structuration of a guarantee facility to support partial credit risk
guarantees for agriculture lending operations, due to specific challenges such as high credit
risks, elevated interest rates and request for collateral requirements which farmers cannot
afford. Women in agriculture being the most financially vulnerable populations, they will
highly benefit from such credit risk guarantees as CRDB Bank will be able to lend money for
projects it wouldn’t have been able to finance otherwise. Moreover, by alleviating collateral
requirements, CRDB Bank will remove the highest burden preventing women from accessing
financial solutions (barrier 2.2.4);
- Technical assistance: Implementation of technical assistance packages and policy
intervention in:
(i) Building institutions’ capacity to implement adaptation measures and adopt climate
resilient agriculture finance;
(ii) Designing and developing climate risks assessment tools to evaluate the viability of
business proposals;
(iii) Training farmers on climate resilient and adaptation solutions;
(iv) Supporting the extension of services in order to apply Good Agricultural Practices in
agriculture;
(v) Supporting policy interventions to attract private sector actors in agriculture resilience
and adaptation lending operations.

=== PAGE 143 ===
Page 140
Outputs (i) and (v) will help structure resilient and adaptation agriculture in Tanzania, reinforcing the
sector and therefore providing better business opportunities for both women and men (barrier 2.2.4).
Outputs (ii), (iii) and (iv) will provide tools, training and support to adopt climate resilient and
adaptation methods, knowledge for which women are more in demand than men, as seen in the
barriers and challenges section (barrier 2.2.3).

Gender Action Plan
I- Methodology
This Gender Action Plan has been developed along with a Gender Assessment as technical annexes for the
Tanzania Agriculture Climate Adaptation Technology Deployment Programme (TACATDP) Funding
Proposal developed by CRDB Bank and submitted to the Green Climate Fund. It is aligned with several of
the GCF’s objectives including with GCF own Gender Action Plan:
▪ Gender -balanced stakeholder consultations at all stages (at least 40%, with a target of 50% for all
consultations);
▪ Objective to promote gender equality in climate action (gender equality at all stages and solutions
tailored to gender specific context, such as more attractive loans or guarantees for women due to their
vulnerable situation);
▪ Women and men will report improvements in their quality of life;
▪ Reduction of the vulnerability of both women and men;
▪ Increase in the adaptive capacity of women and men to respond to the impacts of climate change;
▪ Prevention of economic losses increase in economic opportunities and mitigation of gender-related risks.
II- Objectives
The overall objective of the TACATDP is to support farmers with adaptation tools, knowledge, and capacity
to enable best adaptation options to current and future climatic contexts and agronomic specificities. The
TACATDP explicitly aims to integrate a strong gender lens, ensuring specific targets for women famers
groups and female agribusinesses. More specifically, it intends to provide financial and technical solutions
to farmers, especially women due to their high vulnerability to climate change in Tanzania. Several social
benefits are to be expected, such as an increase in the employment among vulnerable populations of women
and youth.
The TACATDP aims to achieve the following main gender focused results: (i) Equal access to agriculture
financing solutions (loans, guarantees and insurance) in terms of overall amount funded, number of
projects/farms funded, and type of farms funded; (ii) Sensitization of all agriculture value chain
stakeholders, policy makers and financial institutions to gender aspects in resilient agriculture solutions,
and equal opportunities for women and men farmers to adopt diversified, climate-resilient agricultural
options; (iii) Gender responsive and equal participation of women at all stages of the project, during its
design, implementation, and after full implementation (consultations, trainings, awareness raising, etc.);
and (iv) Integration of gender considerations in all the deliverables (parametric insurance, online interfaces,
new/updated policies, manuals, etc.) based on sex-disaggregated data.
Focusing on gender aspects, TACATDP offers balanced opportunities for women’s and men's active
participation. Through a network of microfinance partners and mobile banking facilities, the project will
apply methods and approaches to increase the engagement of small-scale female farm operators and
women-led agribusinesses. The project will make credit available to invest in enterprise expansion,
productivity-enhancing technologies, farm adaptation activities, and improved livelihood strategies. They
will be prioritized in capacity-building activities. It is expected that promoting the use of laborsaving
technologies will help ease the time burden on women; burden they face in their productive capacities. The

=== PAGE 148 ===
Page 145
project will also reduce the burden of unpaid work that often disproportionately falls on women and girls
in every region of the world. The project will further explore and implement an opportunity to address their
access to and control over resources and their decision-making ability over their work burden, opening
greater opportunity for other socio-economic perspectives, including girls' education and women’s
engagement in economic diversification and increase participation in social life. The project will focus on
balancing gender on 50/50 gender balance of potential beneficiaries during project execution.
Finally, all stakeholders involved in the project implementation will have to sign CRDB Gender Policy
upfront and will have to follow and enforce this policy through the whole project. This will ensure that
financial institutions are gender sensitive and that gender responsive actions are embedded in institutional
behaviors and practices, especially in the Technical Assistance and for capacity building components.
In addition, CRDB will put as eligibility criteria the signature of a “statement of non-exclusion criteria”
related to gender policy that will include a disposal for violence against women. If any allegation against a
farmer having received a financial facility from CRDB would occur, that facility will be suspended. In case
the financial facility beneficiary would be condemned by a court for any violence against women, the
financial facility will be stopped immediately with penalties.

=== PAGE 149 ===
Page 146
III- Gender Action Plan
Below is the Gender Action Plan prepared for the TACATDP project:
Activities Indicators and targets Costs* Responsibilities Timeline
Impact Statement: Financial solutions and products (loans, guarantees and insurance) and technical assistance to support development and
adoption of climate change adaptation technologies and solutions. These solutions will increase agriculture resilience in Tanzania, especially for
women which are the most vulnerable populations in Tanzania and do not have equal access to financing and technical solutions as men. They
will therefore promote gender equality in climate action and increase the adaptive capacity of both men and women. The programme will ensure
gender aspects integration at all levels, during the project implementation (consultations, training, awareness raising) and after project
implementation (gender informed deliverables such as policies, manuals and parametric insurance relying on sex-disaggregated data). Gender
balancing will be ensured for CRDB Bank personnel at all stages as well as for all other stakeholders involved in the programme. Finally, the
programme has already integrated gender aspects considerations since design phase, through the preparation of the project concept note and during
the design of activities for the Funding Proposal.
Output statement: At least 40%, with a target of 50%, of both amounts provided through financial support (loans, guarantees and insurance
schemes) and numbers of projects receiving those supports; at least 30% of medium and large farms loans dedicated to female owned farms; at
least 50 funded projects supporting effective adaptation to climate in the agriculture led by female farmers; at least 5 new technologies, practices
or livelihood diversification options, equally available to men and women; 80% of local financial institutions, financing agriculture activities,
sensitized to lending and guarantee providing to female farmers; at least 30% of local financial institutions providing guarantees to female farmers;
100% of female smallholder farmers aware of, and allowed access to insurance schemes; at least 80% of female smallholder farmers accessing
insurance schemes; at least 40%, with a target of 50%, of female stakeholders involved in all trainings, consultations and preparation of
deliverables; at least 40%, with a target of 50%, of the budget spent on activities ensuring gender equality; integration of gender specific aspects
(risks, impacts, considerations, parameters, etc), informed with sex-disaggregated data, in all the deliverables (online interface, extension service
and insurance parametric programmes, policy updating and technical support, etc.); at least 40%, with a target of 50%, of women owned
agribusinesses during capacity building and extension programme activities; creation of at least 10 climate agribusiness incubators providing
equal support to men and women; creation of at least 10 climate agribusiness incubators providing equal support to men and women; at least 30
women climate agribusiness entrepreneurs incubated; promotion of business successes for 10 women on the online interface; at least 40%, with
a 50% target, of CRDB Bank personnel trained through the different training activities being women.
Means of verification: Annual Performance Reports, Interim Evaluation, Completion Report, Final Evaluation.
